Real-world systems kaise scale hote hain, seekho — ye SPA systemdesign42/system-design repo ke poore content ko ek hi page me Hinglish me samjhata hai: 66 real-world case studies (WhatsApp, YouTube, Netflix aur baaki sab), 74 fundamentals, 14 interview designs, 47 AI engineering topics aur 3 foundational white papers tak. Original repo ko dobaara kholne ki jaroorat nahi — har number, har decision aur har trade-off andar hai.

1System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Real-Time Gaming Leaderboard

Gaming leaderboard ek aisi system hai jo lakhon players ke scores ko real-time me rank karke dikhati hai. Har match ke baad score update hota hai, aur leaderboard turant badalni chahiye — delay dikhe to player ko lagega system toota hua hai.

Iska core data structure hai sorted set — Redis ka ZSET iska classic example hai. Har player ka score ek sorted set me hota hai, aur rank nikalna O(log N) ka kaam hai. Score update karte hi rank dobara calculate ho jaata hai, bina poori list dobara sort kiye.

Scale par problem ye hoti hai: top-100 dikhana aasan hai, lekin ek player ki apni rank (jaise 4,32,891st) nikalna mehenga hai. Iske liye score ranges ko buckets me baanta jaata hai, ya periodic snapshots liye jaate hain. Write-heavy games me updates ko pehle message queue me daal kar batch me apply kiya jaata hai, taaki leaderboard reads slow na hon.

  • Core pattern: sorted set (Redis ZSET) + sharded by game/region
  • Key trade-off: strong consistency (har update turant) vs eventual consistency (thoda stale rank, bahut zyada throughput)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: leaderboard read-heavy hoti hai — top ranks cache karo, poori list kabhi scan mat karo
Source:https://systemdesign.one/leaderboard-system-design/
← PehleHomeAgla →Real-Time Live Comments
2System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Real-Time Live Comments

Live comments wo system hai jo live stream ya live event par viewers ke comments ko sabke screen par turant dikhata hai — jaise YouTube live chat ya sports match ka comment feed. Lakhon viewers ek saath comment kar rahe hote hain, aur har comment ko seconds ke andar sab tak pahunchana hota hai.

Architecture ka heart hai pub-sub over WebSockets: viewer ka comment ek message broker (Kafka ya Redis Streams) me jaata hai, wahan se fan-out hota hai, aur connected viewers ko WebSocket connection se push hota hai. Har comment ek baar likha jaata hai, lakhon baar deliver hota hai — isliye write path halka aur read path fan-out par focus hota hai.

Moderation aur spam is system ki sabse badi mushkil hai. Comment aate hi profanity filter, rate limit (ek user kitni baar comment kar sakta hai) aur duplicate detection lagta hai. Popular streams me comments ko sample bhi kiya jaata hai — har comment har viewer ko dikhana zaroori nahi hota, top ya recent comments hi dikhaye jaate hain.

  • Core pattern: WebSocket + pub-sub fan-out, message broker beech me
  • Key trade-off: har comment sabko dikhana (mehenga fan-out) vs sampled/top comments (sasta, acceptable UX)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: live comments me ordering "lagbhag sahi" chalti hai — perfect global order ki koshish system ko slow kar deti hai
Source:https://systemdesign.one/live-comment-system-design/
← PehleReal-Time Gaming LeaderboardAgla →Distributed Counter
3System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Distributed Counter

Distributed counter ek aisa counter hai jo kai machines par chalte hue bhi ek hi sahi number deta hai — jaise video ke views, likes, ya followers count. Ek machine par counter rakhna aasan hai, lekin jab traffic lakhon me ho to ek machine bottleneck ban jaati hai.

Do classic tareeke hain. Pehla: sharded counters — counter ko kai shards me baant do, har shard apna hissa gine, aur total nikalne ke liye sab shards ko jod lo. Dusra: approximate counters — exact number ki jagah lagbhag sahi number dikhao (jaise YouTube views), aur background me exact count sync karte raho.

Consistency yahan sabse bada sawaal hai. Likes ka count agar 2 second purana dikhe to koi fark nahi padta, lekin payment ya inventory ka counter exact hona chahiye. Isliye counter design karne se pehle hamesha poochho: "Kitna stale chalega?" — jawab se pata chalta hai sharding chalega ya strong consistency chahiye.

  • Core pattern: sharded counters + periodic aggregation; ya CRDT-based counters
  • Key trade-off: exact count (slow, strong consistency) vs approximate count (fast, eventually consistent)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: view/like counts ke liye approximate theek hai — write path ko kabhi counter update ke liye block mat karo
Source:https://systemdesign.one/distributed-counter-system-design/
← PehleReal-Time Live CommentsAgla →Real Time Presence Platform
4System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Real Time Presence Platform

Presence platform batati hai ki kaun online hai, kaun typing kar raha hai, kaun kis page par hai — jaise WhatsApp me "online" ya "typing…" dikhta hai, ya Slack me green dot. Ye chhota feature lagta hai, lekin scale par ye sabse mushkil real-time systems me se ek hai.

Har user ke connect/disconnect par ek event banta hai. Ye events ek presence service me aate hain jo in-memory store (Redis) me har user ki latest state rakhti hai. State change hote hi un logon ko push hota hai jo is user ko dekh rahe hain — jaise contacts ya group members. Heartbeat har 20-30 second me aata hai; agar heartbeat ruk jaaye to user ko offline mark kar diya jaata hai.

Scale ki mushkil: 1 billion users × heartbeat har 30 second = 33 million heartbeats per second. Isliye presence state ko sharded in-memory stores me rakha jaata hai, aur sirf "dost list" wale logon ko update bheja jaata hai — poore system ko broadcast nahi kiya jaata.

  • Core pattern: heartbeat + in-memory presence store + targeted fan-out
  • Key trade-off: instant presence (har heartbeat par update — mehenga) vs delayed presence (30-60 second ka grace — sasta)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: presence ke liye "last seen" timestamp hi kaafi hai — har state change ko database me likhne ki jaroorat nahi
Source:https://systemdesign.one/real-time-presence-platform-system-design/
← PehleDistributed CounterAgla →How Virtual Machines Work
5System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Virtual Machines Work

Virtual machine ek aisa software hai jo ek physical computer ke andar ek poora alag computer banata hai — apna CPU, memory, disk aur operating system ke saath. Cloud computing ki neev hi VMs par hai: AWS, GCP aur Azure, sab VMs bechte hain.

VM ka kaam karta hai hypervisor — ek thin software layer jo physical hardware aur VMs ke beech baithta hai. Type-1 hypervisor seedha hardware par chalta hai (jaise AWS Nitro, VMware ESXi), aur Type-2 hypervisor ek OS ke upar chalta hai (jaise VirtualBox). Hypervisor CPU time, memory pages aur disk I/O ko VMs me baant ta hai, aur har VM ko lagta hai ki poora hardware uska hi hai.

VMs ke andar CPU virtualization ke liye hardware support (Intel VT-x, AMD-V) use hota hai, memory ke liye page tables, aur disk ke liye virtual disk images. Containers se farak ye hai: VM me poora guest OS hota hai (bhaari, lekin poori isolation), jabki containers host OS ka kernel share karte hain (halke, lekin kam isolation).

  • Core pattern: hypervisor + hardware-assisted virtualization (VT-x/AMD-V)
  • Key trade-off: VMs — poori isolation, slow start, zyada memory; Containers — fast start, halke, kam isolation
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: cloud me "ek server" matlab aksar ek VM hota hai — uske peeche ek bada physical machine kai VMs me bata hota hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/virtualization-architecture
← PehleReal Time Presence PlatformAgla →How to Scale an App to 10 Million Users on AWS
6System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How to Scale an App to 10 Million Users on AWS

10 million users tak pahunchna ek app ke liye bada milestone hai — aur AWS par iska rasta steps me banta hai. Shuruaat me sab kuch ek server par hota hai: app + database ek hi EC2 instance par. Pehle users ke liye ye kaafi hai, lekin load badhte hi database aur app ko alag karna padta hai.

Doosra step: database ko RDS par le jaana aur app servers ko load balancer (ALB) ke peeche rakhna, taaki app servers ko horizontally badhaya ja sake. Teesra step: static content ko CloudFront CDN par, images ko S3 par, aur frequently-read data ko ElastiCache (Redis) me daalna. Reads database se hat kar cache se aane lagte hain.

10 million users par database hi bottleneck banta hai. Tab read replicas aate hain (reads replicas se, writes primary se), phir sharding (data ko kai databases me baantna), aur writes ko SQS queue se decouple karna. Har step par ek hi rule hai: stateless app servers, managed services, aur har layer par horizontal scaling.

  • Scale ladder: 1 server → ALB + RDS → CDN + S3 + ElastiCache → read replicas → sharding + SQS
  • Key numbers: 10 million users — is scale par database aur cache design hi sab kuch hai
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: pehle stateless bano, phir cache lagao, phir database scale karo — ulta kram hamesha mehenga padta hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/aws-scale
← PehleHow Virtual Machines WorkAgla →This Is How Airbnb Adopted HTTP Streaming to Save 84 Million USD in Costs
7System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

This Is How Airbnb Adopted HTTP Streaming to Save 84 Million USD in Costs

Airbnb ne HTTP streaming adopt karke 84 million USD bachaye — ye ek classic example hai ki protocol-level decision kaise seedha paise se judta hai. Problem ye thi: Airbnb ke pages par bahut saara data server se aata tha, aur purana tareeka har request par poora response ek saath bhejta tha.

HTTP streaming me server response ko chunks me bhejta hai — pehle critical content (jo user ko turant dikhna chahiye), phir baaki hissa. Browser pehla chunk milte hi render shuru kar deta hai, poora response aane ka wait nahi karta. Isse perceived speed badhti hai aur server ko har request par poora payload buffer karke rakhne ki jaroorat nahi padti.

84 million USD ki bachat do jagah se aayi: ek taraf server resources kam lage (kam memory, kam compute per request), aur dusri taraf faster pages se conversion badha — user jaldi book karta hai to revenue badhta hai. Ye case study batati hai ki "critical rendering path" samajhna sirf frontend ki baat nahi hai — ye infrastructure ka paisa hai.

  • Key number: 84 million USD ki bachat — HTTP streaming se
  • Core pattern: chunked transfer — pehle critical content, phir baaki; browser render ka wait nahi karta
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: performance optimization aksar revenue optimization hota hai — iska ROI dollar me naapo, millisecond me nahi
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/what-is-critical-rendering-path
← PehleHow to Scale an App to 10 Million Users on AWSAgla →Amazon Prime Video Microservices Top Failure
8System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Amazon Prime Video Microservices Top Failure

Amazon Prime Video ki microservices architecture ki failure ek famous case study hai — ye batati hai ki microservices har jagah sahi jawab nahi hote. Prime Video ki ek monitoring tool ne video streams ko analyse karne ke liye microservices aur serverless components use kiye the.

Problem ye nikli: har video frame ke liye microservices ke beech bahut saare network calls hote the, aur har call par data ko serialize/deserialize karna padta tha. Isse cost aur latency dono badh gaye. Orchestration (Step Functions) ka kharcha bhi har invocation par lagta tha — aur invocations karodon me the.

Solution ulta tha: microservices ko wapas monolith me merge kar diya gaya. Ek hi process me saara kaam karne se network calls khatam, serialization khatam, aur cost 90% tak kam ho gayi. Ye case study ka sabse bada lesson: microservices ka matlab "har cheez alag service" nahi hai — jahan components ke beech bahut zyada chat hoti hai, wahan monolith hi sahi hai.

  • Core lesson: chatty components + serverless orchestration per call = cost explosion
  • Result: monolith me wapas jaane se cost ~90% kam hui
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: architecture fashion se nahi, data flow se chuno — agar do components har second hazaaron baar baat karte hain to wo ek hi service me hone chahiye
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/prime-video-microservices
← PehleThis Is How Airbnb Adopted HTTP Streaming to Save 84 Million USD in CostsAgla →How Amazon S3 Achieves 99.999999999% Durability
9System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Amazon S3 Achieves 99.999999999% Durability

Amazon S3 ki durability hai 99.999999999% — matlab 11 nines. Iska matlab hai ki agar aap 10 million objects store karo to ek saal me average sirf 0.000001 object khoyega — practically, data kabhi nahi khota. Ye number kaise milta hai, wo S3 ki design me chhupa hai.

S3 har object ko kam se kam 3 alag Availability Zones me replicate karta hai, aur har zone ke andar bhi data kai devices par faila hota hai. Ek disk kharab ho, ek rack jal jaaye, ya ek poora data center band ho jaaye — data phir bhi safe rehta hai kyunki copies alag-alag failure domains me hain.

Iske peeche erasure coding bhi kaam karta hai: data ko chunks me baant kar redundant parity chunks banaye jaate hain, taaki kuch chunks kho bhi jaayein to data dobara banaya ja sake. Saath me continuous integrity checking chalti hai — S3 background me objects ko scan karta hai aur kharab copy milte hi use healthy copy se dobara bana deta hai.

  • Key number: 99.999999999% durability (11 nines) — 10 million objects par saal me ~0.000001 object loss
  • Core pattern: 3+ AZs me replication + erasure coding + continuous integrity checks
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: durability availability se alag hai — data ka khona aur data ka temporarily na milna, do alag cheezein hain
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/amazon-s3-durability
← PehleAmazon Prime Video Microservices Top FailureAgla →How Amazon Lambda Works
10System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Amazon Lambda Works

AWS Lambda ek serverless compute service hai — aap code likhte ho, Lambda use chalata hai, aur aapko server manage nahi karna padta. "Serverless" ka matlab ye nahi ki server nahi hain — matlab hai ki server aapko dikhte nahi, AWS unhe manage karta hai.

Jab koi event aata hai (HTTP request, S3 upload, ya timer), Lambda aapka function chalane ke liye ek execution environment banata hai — ek halka container jisme aapka code aur runtime hota hai. Pehli baar is environment ko banane me time lagta hai — ise cold start kehte hain. Agli baar wahi environment reuse hota hai — warm start, jo bahut fast hota hai.

Lambda ki pricing per-request aur per-millisecond hai — code na chale to paisa nahi lagta. Isliye ye event-driven, bursty workloads ke liye best hai. Lekin lambi chalni wali processes (15 minute ki limit), heavy compute, ya har request par cold start se bachne wale cases me containers ya VMs behtar hote hain.

  • Core pattern: event → execution environment (container) → function run → environment reuse ya destroy
  • Key trade-off: cold start (pehli invocation slow) vs warm start (fast, lekin environment idle rehta hai)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: Lambda ka bill "kitni baar chala × kitni der chala" se banta hai — idle ka paisa nahi lagta, isiliye bursty workloads ke liye best hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/how-does-aws-lambda-work
← PehleHow Amazon S3 Achieves 99.999999999% DurabilityAgla →How Amazon S3 Works
11System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Amazon S3 Works

Amazon S3 (Simple Storage Service) duniya ki sabse badi object storage service hai — isme data objects ke roop me store hota hai, files ke roop me nahi. Har object ek bucket me rehta hai, aur har object ka ek unique key hota hai — jaise "photos/2024/beach.jpg".

S3 ka data model simple hai: object = data + metadata + key. Isme koi directory hierarchy nahi hoti — key me "/" daal kar aap folder jaisa dikhawa kar sakte ho, lekin andar se sab flat hai. Objects ko HTTP API se PUT/GET/DELETE kiya jaata hai, aur har object ka size 5TB tak ho sakta hai.

S3 ke peeche distributed key-value store hai: key ke hash se pata chalta hai data kis partition par hai, aur har partition kai AZs me replicate hota hai. Isi liye S3 practically unlimited scale deta hai — aap jitne objects daalo, system utna hi badhta hai. Versioning, lifecycle rules (purana data saste storage class me bhejna) aur presigned URLs iske sabse use hone wale features hain.

  • Core pattern: flat key-value object store — bucket + key, koi real folders nahi
  • Key numbers: ek object 5TB tak; durability 11 nines; practically unlimited objects
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: S3 file system nahi hai — random access aur rename mehenga hai, lekin massive scale par read/write sasta hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/s3-architecture
← PehleHow Amazon Lambda WorksAgla →How Do Apple AirTags Work
12System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Do Apple AirTags Work

Apple AirTag ek chhota sa tracker hai jo apni location bina GPS aur bina internet ke batata hai. Iska raaz hai Find My network — duniya bhar ke hundreds of millions Apple devices mil kar ek crowd-sourced location network banate hain.

AirTag har 2 second me ek Bluetooth Low Energy beacon bhejta hai, jisme ek rotating public key hoti hai. Aas-paas se guzarne wala koi bhi iPhone is beacon ko sun leta hai, apni GPS location ke saath Apple ke servers ko bhej deta hai — bina ye jaane ki AirTag kiska hai. Aapka iPhone phir us location ko decrypt karke map par dikhata hai.

Privacy ke liye AirTag ki public key har 15 minute me badal jaati hai, taaki koi use track na kar sake. Aur agar koi AirTag aapke saath aapke bina chal raha hai to aapka iPhone alert karta hai — anti-stalking ke liye. Ye design batata hai ki Bluetooth + crowd + cryptography se GPS ke bina bhi location system ban sakta hai.

  • Core pattern: BLE beacon + crowd-sourced relay (paas se guzarne wale devices location report karte hain)
  • Key trade-off: battery (saal bhar chalni chahiye) vs beacon frequency (zyada frequent = zyada accurate, kam battery)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: AirTag khud internet se connect nahi hota — location us network se milti hai jo aas-paas ke devices banate hain
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/how-do-airtags-work
← PehleHow Amazon S3 WorksAgla →How Apple Pay Handles 41 Million Transactions a Day Securely
13System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Apple Pay Handles 41 Million Transactions a Day Securely

Apple Pay har din 41 million transactions handle karta hai — aur iski sabse badi khaasiyat ye hai ki Apple ko aapka card number kabhi milta hi nahi. Security ka poora design is ek principle par hai: asli card details device se bahar kabhi na jaayein.

Jab aap card add karte ho to device me ek Device Account Number banta hai — ek token jo asli card number ki jagah use hota hai. Ye token device ke Secure Enclave me store hota hai, ek alag hardware chip jo OS se bhi isolated hai. Har payment par ek one-time cryptogram banta hai jo sirf us ek transaction ke liye valid hota hai.

Payment karte waqt merchant ko sirf token aur cryptogram milta hai — asli card number kabhi nahi. Agar phone kho jaaye to aap token ko remotely deactivate kar sakte ho, bina card band karwaye. Face ID ya Touch ID se har transaction par biometric confirmation hota hai, taaki chori hua phone bhi payment na kar sake.

  • Key number: 41 million transactions per day
  • Core pattern: tokenization (Device Account Number) + Secure Enclave + one-time cryptogram per transaction
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: asli secret ko kabhi network par mat bhejo — token bhejo, aur token ko bhi har baar naya cryptogram do
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/how-does-apple-pay-work
← PehleHow Do Apple AirTags WorkAgla →How Amazon S3 Achieves Strong Consistency
14System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Amazon S3 Achieves Strong Consistency

S3 ne saalon tak eventual consistency di thi — naya object likhne ke baad turant read karne par kabhi-kabhi purana data ya "not found" milta tha. 2020 me S3 ne strong consistency la di: ab PUT ke baad turant GET karo to naya data hi milega, aur DELETE ke baad object kabhi nahi milega.

Strong consistency ka matlab hai ki har read, latest successful write ko dekhe — chahe request kis bhi AZ se aaye. S3 ne ye apne internal replication protocol ko badal kar haasil kiya: write tabhi successful mana jaata hai jab kaafi replicas ne use accept kar liya ho, aur reads hamesha ek aise replica se serve hote hain jo latest write jaanta ho.

Is change ka asar bahut bada tha: pehle developers ko S3 ke eventual consistency se nipatne ke liye apne code me retry logic, version checks aur extra DynamoDB tables lagane padte the. Strong consistency aane se wo saara workaround khatam ho gaya — S3 ab ek normal strongly-consistent store jaisa behave karta hai, bina performance ya availability khoye.

  • Key change: 2020 se S3 strong consistency deta hai — PUT ke baad turant GET = naya data
  • Core pattern: write tabhi success jab kaafi replicas accept karein; reads hamesha up-to-date replica se
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: strong consistency ka matlab "har read latest" hai — isse application code se saare consistency workarounds hat jaate hain
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/s3-strong-consistency
← PehleHow Apple Pay Handles 41 Million Transactions a Day SecurelyAgla →Bitly URL Shortener Architecture
15System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Bitly URL Shortener Architecture

Bitly URL shortener lambi URLs ko chhoti URLs me badalta hai — jaise ek 200-character wala link "bit.ly/3xK9" ban jaata hai. Kaam simple lagta hai, lekin scale par isme kai interesting decisions hain.

Core flow: user lambi URL deta hai → system ek unique short code banata hai (base62 encoding — a-z, A-Z, 0-9) → code aur original URL ko database me store karta hai. Jab koi short link khole to system code ko lookup karke original URL par 301 redirect karta hai. Sabse tez hissa hai redirect — isliye mapping ko cache (Redis) me rakha jaata hai, taaki har click par database na chhoona pade.

Short code banane ke do tareeke hain: database ka auto-increment ID ko base62 me encode karna (simple, lekin sequential codes guess ho jaate hain), ya random code generate karke collision check karna (secure, lekin retry logic chahiye). Analytics (kitne clicks, kahan se) alag se log kiya jaata hai, taaki redirect path slow na ho.

  • Core pattern: base62 short code + key-value lookup + Redis cache + 301 redirect
  • Key trade-off: sequential IDs (simple, guessable) vs random codes (secure, collision handling chahiye)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: redirect path ko bilkul halka rakho — analytics aur heavy kaam async karo, redirect ko kabhi block mat karo
Source:https://systemdesign.one/url-shortening-system-design/

How Does Bluesky Work

Bluesky ek decentralized social network hai — Twitter jaisa, lekin ek company ke control me nahi. Iska foundation hai AT Protocol, jo social data ko portable banata hai: aapki identity, posts aur follows kisi ek server se bandhe nahi hote.

AT Protocol me har user ka ek DID (Decentralized Identifier) hota hai — ek permanent identity jo aapke domain ya handle se judi hoti hai. Aapka data aapke PDS (Personal Data Server) par rehta hai, aur posts ek standard format (lexicon) me hote hain. Feed, search aur moderation alag services hain jo is data ko read karke apna kaam karti hain — isliye aap server badal kar bhi apni identity aur followers saath le ja sakte ho.

Centralized networks se farak ye hai: Bluesky me koi single point of control nahi hai. Agar ek app band ho jaaye to aapka data aur identity doosre app par chal jaati hai. Iske liye protocol ko open aur data ko portable rakhna padta hai — jo engineering me mushkil hai, kyunki har service ko same data format par agree karna hota hai.

  • Core pattern: AT Protocol — DID identity + PDS data server + alag feed/moderation services
  • Key trade-off: decentralization (portability, koi single control nahi) vs simplicity (centralized system banana aur chalana aasan hai)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: protocol open rakhna hi decentralization hai — data format standard ho to user kabhi lock-in nahi hota

How Canva Supports Real-Time Collaboration for 135 Million Monthly Users

Canva par 135 million monthly users real-time me ek saath design karte hain — do log ek hi design par kaam karein to dono ko turant ek dusre ke changes dikhne chahiye. Ye Google Docs wali problem hai, lekin designs ke liye — jo text se kai guna complex hoti hain.

Canva ke peeche hai RSocket protocol — ek reactive, multiplexed protocol jo ek hi connection par kai streams chala sakta hai. Har design change ek operation ke roop me banta hai, aur ye operations sab collaborators tak order me pahunchte hain. Operations ko apply karne ke liye operational transformation jaisi technique use hoti hai, taaki do log ek saath same element badlein to bhi final state consistent rahe.

Scale par mushkil ye hai ki design ka state bada hota hai — images, fonts, layers. Isliye sirf changes (deltas) bheje jaate hain, poora document nahi. Aur har client apne paas document ka local copy rakhta hai, taaki network slow ho to bhi editing ruk na jaaye — changes baad me sync ho jaate hain.

  • Key number: 135 million monthly users, real-time collaboration
  • Core pattern: RSocket + operation deltas + local-first editing with sync
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: collaboration me sirf changes bhejo, poora document kabhi mat bhejo — aur local copy hamesha rakho taaki editing offline bhi chale

How Cloudflare Was Able to Support 55 Million Requests per Second With Only 15 Postgres Clusters

Cloudflare 55 million requests per second handle karta hai — aur iske peeche sirf 15 Postgres clusters hain. Ye number hairan karta hai, kyunki aam taur par itni traffic ke liye hazaaron database nodes ki kalpana ki jaati hai. Raaz hai: database ko request path se hata dena.

Cloudflare ka request path database ko chhoota hi nahi hai. Edge servers par saara hot data in-memory rehta hai, aur configuration/rules ko periodically sync kiya jaata hai. Postgres sirf control plane ke liye hai — configuration, analytics aur billing ke liye. Data plane (jo har request serve karta hai) memory aur local cache se chalta hai.

Postgres clusters ko bhi bahut soch-samajh kar design kiya gaya hai: har cluster ka ek clear owner hai, connections ko pool kiya jaata hai (PgBouncer), aur reads ko replicas par baanta jaata hai. Lesson ye hai: database ko fast banana hi ekmatra tareeka nahi hai — aksar sahi tareeka ye hota hai ki database ko hot path se hata do.

  • Key numbers: 55 million requests/sec, sirf 15 Postgres clusters
  • Core pattern: data plane (in-memory, edge) vs control plane (Postgres) — alag rakhna
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: sabse tez database wo hai jise request ke waqt chhoona hi na pade — hot data ko memory me rakho, database ko sirf source of truth banao
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/postgresql-scalability

How Disney+ Hotstar Delivered 5 Billion Emojis in Real Time

Disney+ Hotstar ne ek live match me 5 billion emojis real-time me deliver kiye — viewers apne phone par emoji bhej rahe the aur wo sabke screens par turant dikh rahe the. Ye ek aisa scale hai jahan normal chat architecture toot jaata hai.

Problem ye thi: 5 billion individual messages ko har viewer tak pahunchana impossible hai — bandwidth aur processing dono khatam ho jaate. Solution tha aggregation: emojis ko server par count kiya gaya (kaunsa emoji kitni baar), aur viewers ko har second ek aggregated update bheja gaya — "is second me 2.3 million hearts aaye". Viewer ko farak nahi padta ki kisne bheja, bas ye dikhna chahiye ki stadium jhoom raha hai.

Iske liye emoji events ko Kafka jaise stream me daala gaya, wahan windowed aggregation hui (har 1 second ka count), aur result ko edge se broadcast kiya gaya. Individual events kabhi store nahi kiye gaye — sirf counts. Ye pattern har jagah kaam aata hai jahan volume itna zyada ho ki har event deliver karna bekaar ho.

  • Key number: 5 billion emojis, real-time, ek live event me
  • Core pattern: event stream → windowed aggregation → broadcast counts, individual events store mat karo
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: jab volume bahut zyada ho to "kaun" chhodo, "kitne" dikhao — aggregation hi scale ka jawab hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/hotstar-architecture

How Disney+ Hotstar Scaled to 25 Million Concurrent Users

Disney+ Hotstar ne 25 million concurrent users ko ek saath live cricket dikhaya — ye duniya ke sabse bade live-streaming scales me se ek hai. Concurrent matlab ek hi waqt par 25 million log stream dekh rahe hain, aur sabko smooth video chahiye.

Is scale ki neev hai CDN + edge caching: video segments ko pehle se edge servers par cache kiya jaata hai, taaki origin server par load na aaye. Live stream ko chhote segments (2-10 second) me baanta jaata hai, aur har segment ek baar encode hota hai, phir lakhon baar edge se serve hota hai. Isliye cost per viewer bahut kam hoti hai — ek viewer aur judne se origin par koi extra load nahi padta.

Dusri taraf interactive features (scores, emojis, polls) ko video stream se alag rakha gaya — wo alag real-time services se aate hain. Agar interactive service slow ho to bhi video nahi rukti. Capacity planning match ke pehle hoti hai: traffic ka forecast, edge capacity ka pre-warming, aur auto-scaling ke rules pehle se tay hote hain.

  • Key number: 25 million concurrent users, live cricket
  • Core pattern: video segments → CDN edge cache; interactive features alag service se
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: live video me origin ko ek baar encode karne do, phir edge se serve karo — concurrent users badhne se origin load nahi badhta

How Disney+ Scaled to 11 Million Users on Launch Day

Disney+ ne launch day par 11 million users ko handle kiya — ek din me itna traffic jo aam taur par mahinon me banta hai. Launch day ka traffic sabse khatarnak hota hai kyunki wo predictable nahi hota: sab log ek saath aate hain.

Disney+ ki taiyaari me sabse bada hissa tha load testing aur capacity planning: launch se pehle traffic ko simulate karke dekha gaya ki system kahan tootega. Bottlenecks pehle se fix kiye gaye — database connections, cache capacity, aur third-party services (payment, login) ke limits badhaye gaye.

Architecture me feature flags aur graceful degradation the: agar koi service slow ho to non-critical features (recommendations, extra images) band karke core video playback ko bachaya ja sakta tha. Launch day par kuch issues aaye bhi, lekin core playback chalta raha — kyunki system ko is tarah design kiya gaya tha ki partial failure se poora system na gire.

  • Key number: 11 million users, launch day
  • Core pattern: pre-launch load testing + feature flags + graceful degradation
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: launch day par "sab kuch perfect" impossible hai — design aisa karo ki partial failure me bhi core feature chale

How Discord Boosts Performance With Code-Splitting

Discord ne code-splitting se apni app ki performance badhai — ye technique bade JavaScript bundles ko chhote hisson me baant ti hai, taaki browser ko shuruaat me sirf wahi code load karna pade jo turant chahiye.

Problem ye thi: Discord ka web app ek bahut bada single JavaScript bundle tha. User ko app kholne ke liye poora bundle download karna padta tha — slow network par ye seconds lagata tha. Code-splitting me bundle ko routes aur features ke hisaab se baanta gaya: pehle sirf core chat code load hota hai, aur settings, voice, ya video wale parts tab load hote hain jab user unhe khole.

React me ye lazy loading aur dynamic imports se hota hai. Isse initial load time aur Time to Interactive dono kam hote hain. Discord ke liye iska matlab tha: user jaldi chat karna shuru kare, aur baaki features background me aate rahein.

  • Core pattern: route/feature-based code-splitting + dynamic imports (lazy loading)
  • Key trade-off: zyada chunks (pehli load fast, lekin navigation par chhote loads) vs kam chunks (pehli load slow)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: user ko shuruaat me sirf wahi code do jo screen par dikhna hai — baaki sab baad me, zaroorat par

Ye entry README me do jagah hai — Case Study aur Fundamentals dono me — isliye iska ek hi section hai.

How Dropbox Scaled to 100 Thousand Users in a Year After Launch

Dropbox ne launch ke ek saal me 100 thousand users haasil kiye — aur is scale par unki sabse badi mushkil thi file sync ko reliable aur fast rakhna. File sync dikhne me simple hai (file badlo, sab jagah update ho), lekin andar se ye distributed systems ki sabse mushkil problems me se ek hai.

Dropbox ka core hai delta sync: poori file dobara upload karne ki jagah sirf badla hua hissa upload hota hai. File ko blocks me baanta jaata hai, har block ka hash nikalta hai, aur sirf wahi blocks upload hote hain jo badle hain. Isse bandwidth aur time dono bachte hain — badi file me ek line badle to poori file dobara nahi jaati.

Storage ke liye Dropbox ne shuruaat me S3 use kiya, phir apna custom storage system (Magic Pocket) banaya. Metadata (kaunsi file kahan hai, kis version ki hai) ko alag database me rakha gaya, aur file data ko alag storage me — taaki metadata operations fast rahein aur storage independently scale ho.

  • Key number: 100 thousand users, launch ke pehle saal me
  • Core pattern: delta sync (badle hue blocks hi upload) + metadata aur file data ka separation
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: sync me hamesha "kya badla" bhejo, "poori cheez" kabhi mat bhejo — delta hi scale ka raaz hai

How Facebook Scaled Live Video to a Billion Users

Facebook Live ne live video ko ek billion users tak pahunchaya — ye scale video streaming ki sabse badi chunauti hai, kyunki live video ko na pause kiya ja sakta hai aur na pehle se encode karke rakha ja sakta hai.

Live video ka flow hai: broadcaster ka video ingest server par aata hai, wahan real-time me kai qualities me transcode hota hai (1080p, 720p, 480p...), aur phir CDN edge se viewers tak pahunchta hai. Har viewer apne network ke hisaab se quality chunta hai — adaptive bitrate streaming (HLS/DASH) isse possible banata hai.

Scale par sabse badi mushkil hai transcoding capacity: har live stream ko real-time me kai baar encode karna padta hai, aur popular streams par millions of viewers hote hain. Facebook ne iske liye dedicated video processing clusters aur smart routing banaye — popular streams ko zyada resources, aur long-tail streams ko kam. Chat aur reactions ko video se alag service se serve kiya gaya, taaki comment load se video na ruke.

  • Key number: 1 billion users, live video
  • Core pattern: ingest → real-time multi-quality transcode → CDN edge → adaptive bitrate playback
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: live me video aur chat ko alag rakho — chat slow ho to bhi video chalna chahiye, aur video ke liye transcoding capacity hi asli bottleneck hai

How Facebook Was Able to Support a Billion Users via Software Load Balancer

Facebook ne ek billion users ko sirf software load balancer se serve kiya — bina expensive hardware load balancers ke. Ye case study batati hai ki commodity servers par software se bhi massive scale ka load balancing ho sakta hai.

Facebook ka load balancer Katran hai — ye Linux kernel ke XDP (eXpress Data Path) aur IPVS par bana hai. XDP packets ko kernel me bahut jaldi process karta hai, bina userspace me jaaye — isse per-server throughput bahut zyada milta hai. Katran BGP (Border Gateway Protocol) se announce karta hai ki kaunsa VIP kis server par hai, aur ECMP routing traffic ko kai Katran servers me baant deta hai.

Is design ka fayda ye hai: hardware load balancer ki jagah normal servers use hote hain, jo saste hain aur easily scale hote hain. Aur kyunki load balancing kernel level par hota hai, latency bahut kam rehti hai. Ye pattern aaj ke har bade cloud me dikhta hai — software-defined networking ne hardware load balancers ko replace kar diya hai.

  • Key number: 1 billion users, software load balancer se
  • Core pattern: XDP + IPVS (Katran) + BGP/ECMP for traffic distribution
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: load balancing ko kernel ke jitna neeche le jaoge, utni zyada throughput milegi — userspace me har packet bhejna scale par mehenga hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/facebook-load-balancer

How Figma Scaled Postgres to 4M Users

Figma ne Postgres ko 4 million users tak scale kiya — aur ye case study isliye khaas hai kyunki Figma ne database badalne ki jagah Postgres ko hi aur achhe se use karna seekha. Har scale problem ka jawab "naya database" nahi hota.

Figma ka data model ek design file ka hai — bade JSON-like documents jo real-time collaborate hote hain. Pehle Figma ne apne Postgres queries ko optimize kiya: indexes sahi lagaye, hot rows ko pehchana (jo rows har second update hoti hain), aur unhe alag tareeke se handle kiya. Phir read replicas aur connection pooling se throughput badhaya.

Aakhir me Figma ne sharding kiya — data ko kai Postgres instances me baanta, taaki koi ek instance bottleneck na bane. Lekin sharding tab kiya jab zaroorat padi, pehle nahi. Lesson: pehle queries aur indexes theek karo, phir replicas, phir sharding — is kram se saalon tak ek hi database chal sakta hai.

  • Key number: 4 million users, Postgres par
  • Core pattern: query/index optimization → read replicas + pooling → sharding (aakhir me)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: database badalne se pehle use karne ka tareeka badlo — aksar wahi database kaafi hota hai, bas sahi tareeke se use karna hota hai

How Google Search Works

Google Search duniya ka sabse bada information retrieval system hai — har din billions of searches, aur jawab milliseconds me. Iske peeche teen bade systems hain: crawling, indexing, aur ranking.

Crawling me Googlebot web ke pages ko fetch karta hai aur links ko follow karke naye pages dhoondhta hai. Indexing me har page ka content parse hota hai aur ek inverted index banta hai — matlab har word ke saamne un pages ki list jisme wo word hai. Ye index kai machines par sharded hota hai, taaki koi bhi query parallel me lakhon pages ko scan kar sake.

Ranking me query aate hi index se candidate pages nikalte hain, phir hundreds of signals (relevance, PageRank, freshness, user location, page quality) se unhe rank kiya jaata hai. Poora pipeline itna optimize hai ki billions of pages me se best 10 results milliseconds me mil jaate hain.

  • Core pattern: crawl → inverted index (sharded) → ranking signals → top results
  • Key trade-off: freshness (naya content turant index karo) vs completeness (poora web crawl karo — time lagta hai)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: search ka raaz index hai — query ke waqt pages scan nahi hote, pehle se bana index scan hota hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/search-engine-architecture

How Giphy Delivers 10 Billion GIFs a Day to 1 Billion Users

Giphy har din 10 billion GIFs deliver karta hai, 1 billion users ko — aur iska raaz hai CDN. GIFs badi files hoti hain, aur agar har GIF origin server se aati to bandwidth ka kharcha aur latency dono bahut zyada hote.

Giphy ka content CDN edge servers par cache hota hai — user ko GIF uske sabse paas wale edge server se milti hai, origin se nahi. Popular GIFs (trending, viral) to lagbhag hamesha edge par hoti hain, kyunki unhe baar-baar request kiya jaata hai. Isse origin load bahut kam ho jaata hai aur latency milliseconds me rehti hai.

Iske alawa Giphy GIFs ko multiple formats aur sizes me store karta hai — chhota preview, medium, aur full quality. User ke device aur network ke hisaab se sahi size serve hota hai. Mobile par chhoti file, desktop par badi — isse bandwidth bachta hai aur experience fast rehta hai.

  • Key numbers: 10 billion GIFs/day, 1 billion users
  • Core pattern: CDN edge caching + multiple sizes/formats per GIF
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: media delivery me origin ko sirf pehli baar chhoona chahiye — uske baad edge hi serve kare

How to Scale an App to 100 Million Users on GCP

100 million users GCP par — ye AWS wale 10-million-user case study ka agla step hai, aur yahan architecture ke decisions aur bhi sakht hote hain. GCP par is scale ka rasta hai: Cloud Load Balancing → GKE (Kubernetes) → Cloud Spanner / Cloud SQL → Memorystore → Cloud CDN.

100 million users par sab kuch multi-region hona chahiye — ek region down ho to dusra region traffic sambhale. Data ke liye Cloud Spanner (globally distributed, strongly consistent) ya sharded Cloud SQL use hota hai. Stateless services GKE par chalte hain, aur auto-scaling traffic ke hisaab se pods badhata-ghatata hai.

Is scale par cost optimization bhi design ka hissa ban jaata hai: committed use discounts, preemptible VMs for batch work, aur data transfer costs ko kam rakhne ke liye region-wise data placement. 10 million se 100 million ka safar sirf "aur servers" nahi hai — ye multi-region, cost-aware design ka safar hai.

  • Key number: 100 million users, GCP par
  • Core pattern: multi-region + GKE autoscaling + globally distributed database + CDN
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: 100M par har decision me cost bhi hoti hai — architecture jo scale kare aur bill bhi control me rakhe, wahi sahi hai

How Google Docs Works

Google Docs me kai log ek hi document ko ek saath edit karte hain, aur sabko ek dusre ke changes turant dikhte hain — bina kabhi conflict ke. Ye real-time collaboration ki sabse purani aur sabse successful implementation hai.

Iske peeche hai Operational Transformation (OT): har edit ek operation hota hai (jaise "position 42 par 'x' insert karo"). Jab do log ek saath edit karte hain to unke operations ko transform kiya jaata hai — matlab operations ko is tarah adjust kiya jaata hai ki final document sabke paas same ho, chahe operations kis order me aayein.

Google Docs ka server operations ko order karta hai aur sab clients ko broadcast karta hai. Har client ke paas document ka local copy hota hai, aur operations aate hi local copy update ho jaata hai. Agar network tut jaaye to bhi editing chalti rehti hai — operations queue me rehte hain aur reconnect par sync ho jaate hain.

  • Core pattern: Operational Transformation (OT) + central operation ordering + local copies
  • Key trade-off: OT (complex, lekin precise) vs CRDT (simpler convergence, lekin bada state)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: collaboration me "operations" bhejo, "poora document" nahi — aur operations ka order server tay kare

How Hashnode Generates Feed at Scale

Hashnode ka feed har user ko relevant blog posts dikhata hai — aur scale par feed generation sabse mushkil problems me se ek hai, kyunki har user ka feed alag hota hai aur har second naye posts aate hain.

Feed banane ke do tareeke hain: fan-out on write (post aate hi sab followers ke feed me daal do — read fast, write mehenga) aur fan-out on read (user feed khole tab follow kiye hue logon ke posts nikaal kar rank karo — write sasta, read mehenga). Hashnode jaise medium-scale systems hybrid use karte hain: normal users ke liye fan-out on write, aur bahut bade followings (celebrities) ke liye fan-out on read.

Feed ko rank karne ke liye recency, engagement (likes, comments) aur user ki interests use hoti hain. Feed ko pre-compute karke cache me rakha jaata hai, taaki har feed request par database query na karni pade. Naye posts aate hi cache invalidation ya incremental update hota hai.

  • Core pattern: hybrid fan-out (write + read) + pre-computed ranked feed + cache
  • Key trade-off: fan-out on write (read fast, celebrity problem) vs fan-out on read (write sasta, read slow)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: celebrity (lakhon followers) ke posts ko fan-out mat karo — unhe read time par merge karo
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/feed-architecture
← PehleHow Google Docs WorksAgla →How Halo Scaled to 11.6 Million Users Using the Saga Design Pattern
32System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Halo Scaled to 11.6 Million Users Using the Saga Design Pattern

Halo ne 11.6 million users tak scale kiya Saga design pattern se — ye pattern distributed transactions ki problem ka jawab hai. Jab ek kaam kai services me faila ho (jaise order → payment → inventory → shipping), aur beech me ek step fail ho jaaye to poora kaam wapas kaise lein?

Saga me har step ke saath ek compensating action hoti hai — matlab har kaam ko undo karne ka tareeka. Order ban gaya, payment fail hui → compensating action order ko cancel karti hai. Payment ho gayi, inventory fail hui → payment refund hoti hai. Har service apna kaam aur apna undo dono jaanti hai.

Do tareeke hain saga chalane ke: choreography (har service event bhejti hai, agli service sun kar aage badhti hai — simple, lekin flow samajhna mushkil) aur orchestration (ek central orchestrator steps ko chalata hai aur fail par compensations trigger karta hai — control zyada, lekin orchestrator ek extra component hai).

  • Key number: 11.6 million users, Saga pattern se
  • Core pattern: distributed transaction = steps + har step ki compensating (undo) action
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: microservices me "ACID transaction" nahi hoti — saga se eventual consistency haasil karo, aur har step ka undo pehle se likho
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/saga-design-pattern

How Instagram Scaled to 2.5 Billion Users

Instagram 2.5 billion users tak pahuncha — duniya ke sabse bade social networks me se ek. Is scale ki kahani me sabse bada lesson hai: shuruaat me simple stack, aur scale ke saath ek-ek karke bottlenecks hatana.

Shuruaat me Instagram Django + Postgres par chala — ek hi database, ek hi app. Users badhe to pehle read replicas aaye, phir Memcached (cache), phir sharding (users ko kai databases me baantna). Photos ke liye S3 + CDN use hua, aur feed ke liye fan-out on write (post aate hi followers ke feed me daal do).

Instagram ki khaas baat hai media pipeline: har upload hone wali photo/video ko kai sizes me transcode kiya jaata hai, aur ML se content moderation hoti hai. Stories aur Reels ne architecture me aur complexity jodi — Reels ke liye recommendation system (ranking) feed se alag aur bahut zyada compute-heavy hai.

  • Key number: 2.5 billion users
  • Core pattern: Django + Postgres → replicas → Memcached → sharding → S3/CDN → fan-out feed
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: scale ek din me nahi aata — har bottleneck ko uske time par hatana hi scaling hai; shuruaat me over-engineering mat karo

How Khan Academy Scaled to 30 Million Users

Khan Academy ne 30 million users ko free education di — aur iski scale story me sabse interesting baat ye hai ki ye ek non-profit hai, isliye cost control architecture ka pehla requirement tha. Har dollar bachta hai to aur students ko padhaya ja sakta hai.

Khan Academy ka content mostly static aur cacheable hai — videos aur exercises baar-baar same hote hain. Isliye CDN par aggressive caching ki gayi: video aur static assets edge se serve hote hain, origin par load bahut kam aata hai. Interactive parts (progress tracking, exercises) ko alag services me rakha gaya, taaki video traffic se wo slow na hon.

Database ke liye sharding aur read replicas use hue, lekin sabse bada lever tha cache hit ratio — 95%+ requests cache se serve hoti hain. Isse origin servers kam chahiye, aur bill bhi kam aata hai. Education platforms ke liye ye lesson hai: content ko pehchano jo kabhi nahi badalta, aur use CDN par daal do.

  • Key number: 30 million users, non-profit — cost control pehli priority
  • Core pattern: static content → aggressive CDN caching; interactive parts alag services me
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: jo content kabhi nahi badalta, use origin se kabhi mat serve karo — CDN hi uska ghar hai

How LinkedIn Scaled to 930 Million Users

LinkedIn 930 million users tak pahuncha — professional network jisme profiles, connections, feed, jobs, aur messaging sab ek saath chalte hain. Is scale par har feature ka apna data model aur apni scaling story hai.

LinkedIn ki architecture me graph central hai — logon ke beech connections ek graph hain, aur "2nd degree connections" jaise queries graph traversals hain. Feed ke liye fan-out on write use hota hai, aur search ke liye Galene/Lucene-based inverted index. Har service apna datastore chunti hai: profile ke liye key-value, feed ke liye time-series jaisa store, aur graph queries ke liye specialized index.

LinkedIn ne kai internal systems open source kiye hain — Kafka, Samza, Venice, Pinot — jo iski scaling journey ke saboot hain. Inme se Kafka to aaj poori industry ka standard hai. Lesson: scale par ek database se kaam nahi chalta — har access pattern ke liye sahi datastore chuno.

  • Key number: 930 million users
  • Core pattern: graph data model + fan-out feed + har access pattern ke liye alag datastore (Kafka, Venice, Pinot)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: ek database sab kuch nahi kar sakta — profile, feed, search aur graph ke liye alag-alag stores chahiye

How LinkedIn Adopted Protocol Buffers to Reduce Latency by 60%

LinkedIn ne Protocol Buffers (protobuf) adopt karke apni latency 60% tak kam ki — ye ek chhota sa change tha jiska asar bahut bada tha. Problem thi JSON: har service ke beech data JSON me jaata tha, jo text-based hai — bada, slow parse hone wala.

Protobuf ek binary serialization format hai: data ko compact binary me encode kiya jaata hai, jo JSON se 3-10 guna chhota hota hai aur parse karne me bahut fast hota hai. Schema pehle se define hoti hai (.proto file), to sender aur receiver dono ko pata hota hai data ka structure — validation aur versioning bhi aasan ho jaati hai.

LinkedIn ne apne internal APIs ko JSON se protobuf par migrate kiya — pehle high-traffic services, phir baaki. 60% latency kam hone ka matlab hai ki har request pehle se aadhe se bhi kam time me poori hoti hai, aur network bandwidth bhi bahut bachta hai. Microservices me services ke beech har millisecond count hoti hai, aur serialization wahi millisecond khaata hai.

  • Key number: latency 60% kam hui — protobuf se
  • Core pattern: JSON (text, bada, slow parse) → protobuf (binary, compact, fast parse, schema-based)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: services ke beech data format bhi performance hai — JSON se binary par jaana aksar sabse sasta optimization hota hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/protocol-buffers-vs-json

Tech Stack Evolution at Levels fyi

Shuruaat me sab kuch ek Google Sheet + simple website par chala — data Sheet me, site static.

Users badhe to Sheet bottleneck ban gayi: concurrent edits, data validation, aur query performance — sab me dikkat aane lagi. Tab team ne proper database (Postgres) + API layer par migrate kiya, aur frontend ko modern framework par le gayi. Har migration tab hui jab purana tareeka tootne laga, pehle nahi.

Is case study ka lesson hai evolutionary architecture: stack ko pehle din se perfect banane ki koshish mat karo. Simple shuru karo, jab bottleneck dikhe tab badlo, aur har badlav ko data se justify karo — "Sheet slow ho rahi hai" ek achha reason hai, "nayi technology cool hai" nahi.

  • Core pattern: Google Sheets + static site → Postgres + API + modern frontend (jab zaroorat padi, tab)
  • Key trade-off: simple stack (fast start, jaldi toottega) vs heavy stack (slow start, der tak chalega)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: stack ko tab badlo jab purana tootte lage — pehle nahi; har migration ka reason ek real bottleneck hona chahiye

How Lyft Support Rides to 21 Million Users

Lyft 21 million users ko rides deta hai — aur ride-hailing ki sabse mushkil problem hai real-time matching: sahi driver ko sahi rider se milana, sahi waqt par, sahi jagah par. Ye ek location + time + supply-demand optimization problem hai.

Lyft ki architecture me geospatial indexing central hai — drivers aur riders ki locations ko geohash ya S2 cells me baanta jaata hai, taaki "paas wale drivers" nikalna fast ho. Matching ke liye dispatch service har naye ride request par nearby drivers ko dhoondhti hai, unki ETA nikaalti hai, aur best match chunti hai. Pricing (surge) ke liye supply aur demand ko real-time me measure kiya jaata hai.

Scale par sabse badi mushkil hai write volume: har driver har kuch second me apni location bhejta hai — lakhon drivers × har 3 second = tens of thousands of writes per second. Ye writes ek high-throughput stream (Kafka) me jaate hain, aur matching service in-memory state se kaam karti hai, database se nahi — kyunki matching ko milliseconds me jawab chahiye.

  • Key number: 21 million users, real-time ride matching
  • Core pattern: geohash/S2 spatial index + dispatch service + in-memory matching state (Kafka stream se)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: location-based matching me database ko beech me mat laao — in-memory state + stream hi milliseconds de sakta hai

How McDonald’s Food Delivery Platform Handles 20,000 Orders per Second

McDonald's ka food delivery platform 20,000 orders per second handle karta hai — lunch aur dinner ke peak par to aur bhi zyada. Food delivery me order ka flow hai: order → restaurant → kitchen → driver → customer — aur har step par real-time tracking chahiye.

Is scale par architecture event-driven hai: har order ek event hai jo Kafka jaisi stream me jaata hai, aur alag-alag services (restaurant, kitchen display, driver dispatch, customer tracking) us event ko sun kar apna kaam karti hain. Koi service dusri ka wait nahi karti — sab async chalta hai, isliye ek service slow ho to bhi order flow nahi rukta.

Peak load ke liye autoscaling + queue buffering use hota hai: achanak orders badhein to queue unhe absorb karti hai, aur workers scale hokar queue ko drain karte hain. Restaurant aur driver ki capacity bhi real-time me track hoti hai, taaki orders usi ko milein jo handle kar sake — nahi to customer ko late delivery aur restaurant ko overload, dono hote.

  • Key number: 20,000 orders per second
  • Core pattern: event-driven (Kafka) + async services + queue buffering for peaks
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: food delivery me har step async hona chahiye — ek slow step poora order flow nahi rokna chahiye

40System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Meta Achieves 99.99999999% Cache Consistency

Meta 99.99999999% cache consistency achieve karta hai — matlab cache me data galat hone ki probability 100 million me se 1 se bhi kam hai. Ye number hairan karta hai kyunki cache aur database ke beech consistency rakhna distributed systems ki sabse mushkil problems me se ek hai.

Meta ka cache hai Memcached (Mcrouter + TAO) — aur consistency ka raaz hai lease-based invalidation: jab data badalta hai to cache entry ko turant invalidate kiya jaata hai, aur lease mechanism se ye pakka kiya jaata hai ki stale read na ho. Agar invalidation message kho jaaye to lease expire hone par entry apne aap refresh hoti hai — isse worst-case staleness bounded rehti hai.

Iske alawa Meta write-through aur read-through patterns ko carefully combine karta hai, aur cache ko database ka "source of truth" kabhi nahi banata — database hamesha final authority rehti hai. Cache sirf speed ke liye hai, correctness ke liye nahi. Ye distinction hi 8 nines ka raaz hai.

  • Key number: 99.99999999% cache consistency (8 nines)
  • Core pattern: lease-based invalidation + bounded staleness + database as source of truth
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: cache ko kabhi source of truth mat banao — cache sirf speed hai, correctness hamesha database se aani chahiye
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/cache-consistency

How Does Netflix Work?

Netflix duniya ki sabse badi video streaming service hai — aur iska architecture microservices ka sabse famous example hai. Netflix par har user ko personalized home page, recommendations, aur smooth video chahiye — sab ek saath, duniya bhar me.

Netflix ka flow hai: user request Zuul (API gateway) par aati hai, wahan se hundreds of microservices me jaati hai — recommendations, search, billing, playback, aur personalization, sab alag services. Video ke liye Netflix apna Open Connect CDN chalata hai — ISPs ke data centers me Netflix ke cache servers lage hote hain, taaki video user ke bahut paas se serve ho.

Video ko per-title encoding se optimize kiya jaata hai: har title (movie/show) ke liye alag bitrate ladder banti hai, kyunki animation aur action movie ki bandwidth needs alag hoti hain. Isse bandwidth bachta hai aur quality badhti hai. Aur sabse important: Netflix ne chaos engineering invent ki — apne hi system ko tod kar test karna, taaki real failure par system na gire.

  • Core pattern: API gateway (Zuul) + hundreds of microservices + Open Connect CDN + per-title encoding
  • Key trade-off: microservices (independent scaling, lekin operational complexity) vs monolith (simple, lekin scaling mushkil)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: video me sabse bada cost bandwidth hai — per-title encoding aur edge caching hi iska jawab hai

42System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Microservices Lessons From Netflix

Netflix se microservices ke sabse bade lessons mile hain — kyunki Netflix ne microservices ko sabse pehle aur sabse bade scale par adopt kiya tha. Unke lessons me sabse pehla hai: microservices free nahi hote — har service ka operational cost hota hai (deployment, monitoring, debugging).

Doosra lesson: failure isolation — ek service fail ho to dusri chalni chahiye. Netflix ne iske liye Hystrix (circuit breaker) banaya: agar koi downstream service slow ho to circuit open ho jaata hai aur fallback response milta hai, taaki poora request fail na ho. Teesra lesson: observability — hundreds of services me bina distributed tracing ke debugging impossible hai.

Chautha lesson: microservices me data ownership clear honi chahiye — har service ka apna datastore, aur services ke beech data share karne ke liye events, direct database access nahi. Ye lessons aaj har microservices team ke liye basic rules hain.

  • Core lessons: failure isolation (circuit breaker) + observability (distributed tracing) + data ownership (events, not shared DB)
  • Key trade-off: microservices ki azadi vs operational cost — har service ka monitoring aur deployment cost hota hai
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: microservices me sabse pehle circuit breaker aur tracing lagao — bina inke scale par debugging impossible hai

Ye entry README me do jagah hai — Case Study aur Fundamentals dono me — isliye iska ek hi section hai.

How Netflix Uses Chaos Engineering to Create Resilience Systems

Netflix ne chaos engineering invent ki — apne production system me jaan-boojh kar failure daal kar dekhna ki system kaise behave karta hai. Iska sabse famous tool hai Chaos Monkey, jo randomly servers ko band kar deta hai.

Chaos engineering ka principle hai: failure to hogi hi — behtar hai wo aapki marzi se, controlled tareeke se ho, taaki aapko pata chale system kahan kamzor hai. Chaos Monkey ke baad Netflix ne Chaos Kong (poora region down karna), Latency Monkey (network delay daalna) aur Chaos Gorilla jaise tools banaye.

Is practice se Netflix ka system itna resilient bana ki real failures (server crash, region outage) par bhi users ko pata nahi chalta — traffic automatically healthy servers par shift ho jaata hai. Aaj chaos engineering har bade system ki standard practice hai, aur iska core idea hai: resilience test karke banti hai, assume karke nahi.

  • Core pattern: controlled failure injection (Chaos Monkey → Kong → Latency Monkey) in production
  • Key principle: failure ko pehle se test karo — jo failure tumne test nahi ki, wahi tumhe sabse zyada nuksaan pahunchayegi
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: resilience ek feature hai jo test karni padti hai — "system kabhi fail nahi hoga" ye sochna hi sabse badi galti hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/chaos-engineering
← PehleMicroservices Lessons From NetflixAgla →How PayPal Was Able to Support a Billion Transactions per Day With Only 8 Virtual Machines
44System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How PayPal Was Able to Support a Billion Transactions per Day With Only 8 Virtual Machines

PayPal ne ek billion transactions per day ko sirf 8 virtual machines se handle kiya — ye number isliye possible hua kyunki unhone Actor Model use kiya. Actor Model ek concurrency model hai jisme har "actor" ek independent unit hota hai jo sirf messages se baat karta hai.

Actor Model me har actor ka apna state aur apna mailbox hota hai. Actors ek dusre ka state directly access nahi karte — sirf messages bhejte hain. Isse locks ki zaroorat hi khatam ho jaati hai, aur system millions of actors ko ek saath chala sakta hai, kyunki actors bahut halke hote hain (threads se hazaaron guna halke).

PayPal ne is model ko apne payment processing me lagaya: har transaction ek actor ke through process hoti hai, aur actors ke beech message passing se throughput bahut zyada milti hai. 8 VMs par 1 billion transactions ka matlab hai har VM par ~125 million transactions per day — ye sirf isliye possible hai kyunki actor model me context switching aur locking ka overhead lagbhag zero hai.

  • Key numbers: 1 billion transactions/day, sirf 8 virtual machines
  • Core pattern: Actor Model — lightweight actors + message passing, no shared state, no locks
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: concurrency ka sabse bada kharcha locks aur shared state hai — use hata do to throughput kai guna badh jaati hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/actor-model
← PehleHow Netflix Uses Chaos Engineering to Create Resilience SystemsAgla →Pastebin
45System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Pastebin

Pastebin ek simple service hai: text paste karo, ek link pao, aur use share karo. Interview me ye sabse zyada poochha jaane wala system design question hai — kyunki isme core concepts (storage, expiry, scaling) sab ek saath aate hain.

Design ka flow: user text bhejta hai → server ek unique key banata hai (random ya hash-based) → text ko object storage (S3 jaisa) me store karta hai aur key-to-location mapping ko database me → user ko short URL milta hai. Read karte waqt key se mapping nikaal kar object serve kiya jaata hai — popular pastes ko cache me rakha jaata hai.

Expiry aur cleanup is system ki khaas baat hai: pastes ki expiry hoti hai (10 minute, 1 din, kabhi nahi), aur expired pastes ko background job delete karta hai. Analytics aur spam control ke liye rate limiting lagta hai. Scale par sabse bada sawal hota hai: metadata database ko kaise shard karein, aur hot pastes (viral) ko cache se kaise serve karein.

  • Core pattern: unique key + object storage for text + metadata DB + cache for hot pastes
  • Key trade-off: key generation — random (secure, collision check) vs sequential hash (simple, guessable)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: Pastebin me text aur metadata ko alag rakho — text object store me, mapping database me; expiry ke liye background cleanup job chahiye
Source:https://systemdesign.one/system-design-pastebin/

Designing a Payment Backend with Stripe Integration

Payment backend design karna system design ka sabse sensitive kaam hai — yahan galti ka matlab hai paise ka nuksaan. Stripe integration ke saath payment backend ka core flow hai: checkout → payment intent → charge → confirmation, aur har step par idempotency aur reconciliation chahiye.

Stripe ka model hai: aapka backend card details ko kabhi touch nahi karta — Stripe.js ya Payment Element card ko seedha Stripe tak bhejta hai, aur aapko ek payment method token milta hai. Aapka backend is token se PaymentIntent banata hai, aur Stripe charge karta hai. Webhooks se aapko payment ki final status milti hai — kyunki charge hone me time lag sakta hai.

Is design me sabse important hain: idempotency keys (double charge se bachna), webhook signature verification (fake webhook se bachna), aur reconciliation (apne database aur Stripe ke records ko roz milana). Payment me "lagbhag sahi" nahi chalta — har paisa hisaab me hona chahiye.

  • Core pattern: Stripe Elements (card tokenization) → PaymentIntent → webhook confirmation → reconciliation
  • Key trade-off: synchronous confirmation (simple UX, lekin slow) vs async webhooks (fast UX, lekin state machine chahiye)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: payment me idempotency key aur webhook verification non-negotiable hain — bina inke double charge ya fraud pakka hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/design-a-payment-system

This Is How Quora Shards MySQL to Handle 13+ Terabytes

Quora ne MySQL ko shard karke 13+ terabytes data handle kiya — ye case study batati hai ki relational database bhi massive scale par chal sakta hai, bas sharding sahi tareeke se karni hoti hai. Quora ka data model hai: questions, answers, users, votes, aur follows — sab relational.

Quora ki sharding strategy hai range + hash hybrid: data ko logical shards me baanta gaya hai, aur har shard ek MySQL instance (ya cluster) par hai. Shard key ko is tarah chuna gaya hai ki related data ek hi shard par rahe — taaki zyada queries single-shard hon aur cross-shard joins kam se kam hon.

Cross-shard queries se bachne ke liye Quora ne denormalization kiya — kuch data ko duplicate karke rakha, taaki har shard apne sawaalon ke jawab khud de sake. Aur reads ke liye read replicas + cache (Memcached) use hua. Lesson: sharding sirf data baantna nahi hai — access patterns ko samajh kar shard key chunna hi asli kaam hai.

  • Key number: 13+ terabytes, MySQL sharded
  • Core pattern: shard key jo related data ko ek shard par rakhe + denormalization + read replicas + cache
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: sharding se pehle access patterns samjho — galat shard key se har query cross-shard ban jaati hai aur sharding ka fayda khatam
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/mysql-sharding
← PehleDesigning a Payment Backend with Stripe IntegrationAgla →How Razorpay Scaled to Handle Flash Sales at 1500 Requests per Second

How Razorpay Scaled to Handle Flash Sales at 1500 Requests per Second

Razorpay ne flash sales ke dauran 1500 requests per second handle kiye — flash sale ka traffic sabse khatarnak hota hai kyunki wo ek hi pal me aata hai: sale shuru hote hi hazaaron log ek saath payment karte hain.

Payment gateway ke liye flash sale me sabse bada risk hai double charge aur timeout: user ko lage payment fail hui, wo dobara try kare, aur dono baar paise kat jaayein. Isse bachne ke liye Razorpay ne idempotency keys aur request deduplication lagaya — same request dobara aaye to pehle wala result hi wapas milta hai.

Scale ke liye async processing + queue use hua: payment request aate hi turant accept karke queue me daal di jaati hai, aur workers use process karte hain. User ko turant "processing" dikhta hai, aur final status webhook/polling se milti hai. Isse gateway kabhi overload nahi hota — queue hi buffer ka kaam karti hai.

  • Key number: 1500 requests/second, flash sale peak
  • Core pattern: idempotency keys + async queue processing + webhook status
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: payment me peak traffic ko queue se absorb karo — synchronous processing par flash sale hi gateway ko gira dega

49System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Reddit Works

Reddit ek massive community platform hai — thousands of subreddits, millions of posts aur comments, aur voting se content rank hota hai. Reddit ki architecture me sabse interesting hissa hai ranking aur feed generation.

Reddit ka feed "hot" algorithm se banta hai: post ka score (upvotes − downvotes) aur uski age — naye posts ko boost milta hai, purane posts neeche jaate hain. Har subreddit ka apna feed hota hai, aur home feed ke liye subscribed subreddits ke top posts merge hote hain. Voting data ko Cassandra jaise wide-column store me rakha jaata hai, kyunki votes ka volume bahut zyada hai aur writes ko fast hona chahiye.

Comments ke liye tree structure use hoti hai — har comment ka parent hota hai, aur threads ko efficiently fetch karne ke liye materialized paths ya nested sets use hote hain. Scale par Reddit ne monolith se microservices ki taraf kadam badhaya, lekin sabse bada lesson ye hai: voting aur ranking ko cache aur pre-compute karo, har request par calculate mat karo.

  • Core pattern: hot ranking (score + age) + Cassandra for votes + pre-computed feeds
  • Key trade-off: real-time ranking (fresh, lekin mehenga) vs pre-computed ranking (fast, lekin thoda stale)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: voting jaise high-write features ke liye write-optimized store chuno, aur ranking ko pre-compute karo
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/reddit-architecture
← PehleHow Razorpay Scaled to Handle Flash Sales at 1500 Requests per SecondAgla →Slack Architecture
50System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Slack Architecture

Slack ek team communication platform hai — channels, direct messages, threads, aur integrations, sab real-time me. Slack ki architecture me sabse important hai real-time messaging aur workspace isolation.

Slack ka message flow hai: user message bhejta hai → message gateway use accept karta hai → message ko channel ke datastore me likha jaata hai → phir pub-sub se un sab clients tak push hota hai jo us channel ko dekh rahe hain. WebSockets se har client ka persistent connection rehta hai, taaki messages turant mil sakein.

Scale par Slack ne workspace-based sharding kiya: har workspace (company) ka data alag shard par hai, taaki ek bade workspace ka load dusre ko affect na kare. Search ke liye alag index (Solr/Elasticsearch) hai, aur files ke liye object storage. Presence (kaun online hai) ke liye alag real-time service hai — kyunki presence ka data model message se bilkul alag hai.

  • Core pattern: WebSocket gateway + pub-sub fan-out + workspace-based sharding + separate search index
  • Key trade-off: workspace sharding (isolation achhi, lekin cross-workspace features mushkil) vs global sharding
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: chat me message, presence aur search ko alag services me rakho — inke data models aur scale patterns bilkul alag hain
Source:https://systemdesign.one/slack-architecture/

51System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Virtual Waiting Room Architecture That Handles High-Demand Ticket Sales at SeatGeek

SeatGeek ka virtual waiting room high-demand ticket sales ko handle karta hai — jaise popular concert ke tickets khulte hi lakhon log ek saath aate hain. Bina waiting room ke, itna traffic ticketing system ko gira deta hai aur genuine buyers ko tickets nahi milte.

Waiting room ka kaam hai traffic ko queue me daal kar controlled rate par andar bhejna: user aata hai → use queue me ek position milti hai → jab uski baari aati hai to use ticketing site par bheja jaata hai. Queue position ko token ya signed URL se verify kiya jaata hai, taaki koi queue jump na kar sake.

Is design me sabse important hai fairness aur anti-bot: bots ko queue me aane se rokna (CAPTCHA, behavior analysis), aur genuine users ko unki jagah ka sahi estimate dena ("aapki baari 12 minute me"). Queue ko edge par chalaya jaata hai (CDN/workers), taaki origin par load hi na aaye — waiting room khud hi sabse bada load absorber hai.

  • Core pattern: edge-based queue + tokenized entry + controlled admission rate
  • Key trade-off: strict FIFO (fair, lekin slow) vs prioritized/lottery (fast, lekin fairness sawal)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: flash sale me sabse pehle traffic ko rokna seekho — queue hi tumhara sabse sasta load balancer hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/virtual-waiting-room

How Shopify Handles Flash Sales at 32 Million Requests per Minute

Shopify ne flash sales me 32 million requests per minute handle kiye — ye duniya ke sabse bade e-commerce peaks me se ek hai. Flash sale ka traffic normal traffic se 100 guna zyada ho sakta hai, aur wo bhi ek hi minute me.

Shopify ki architecture me sabse bada change tha modular monolith: pehle pure monolith se microservices ki taraf gaye, phir wapas modular monolith par aaye — kyunki flash sale me services ke beech ke network calls hi bottleneck ban gaye the. Modular monolith me modules alag hain lekin ek hi process me chalte hain — isse cross-module calls fast hote hain.

Flash sale ke liye checkout ko isolate kiya gaya hai: checkout ka path baaki store se alag hai, taaki browsing traffic se checkout slow na ho. Inventory ke liye reservation system hai — item cart me aate hi inventory reserve hoti hai, taaki overselling na ho. Aur reads ke liye aggressive caching + read replicas use hote hain.

  • Key number: 32 million requests per minute, flash sale peak
  • Core pattern: modular monolith + isolated checkout path + inventory reservation + aggressive caching
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: flash sale me checkout ko sabse zyada bachao — browsing slow ho to chalega, lekin checkout slow hua to sale gayi

← PehleVirtual Waiting Room Architecture That Handles High-Demand Ticket Sales at SeatGeekAgla →This Is How Stripe Does Rate Limiting to Build Scalable APIs
53System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

This Is How Stripe Does Rate Limiting to Build Scalable APIs

Stripe ne rate limiting ko apni API design ka core hissa banaya — kyunki ek public API par koi bhi client galti se ya jaan-boojh kar itne requests bhej sakta hai ki poora system slow ho jaaye. Stripe ka rate limiting per-account aur per-endpoint hai.

Stripe token bucket algorithm use karta hai: har account ke paas tokens ka bucket hota hai, har request ek token khata hai, aur bucket ek fixed rate par refill hota hai. Burst requests bucket se handle hote hain, lekin sustained overload par 429 (Too Many Requests) milta hai — saath me Retry-After header bhi, taaki client ko pata chale kab dobara try kare.

Rate limiting sirf abuse se bachne ke liye nahi hai — ye fairness ke liye bhi hai: ek bhaari client dusre clients ka quota na kha jaaye. Stripe ke headers me rate limit ki status bhi milti hai (kitne requests bache hain), taaki clients khud apni speed adjust kar sakein. Ye API design ka best practice hai: limits ko chhupao mat, dikhao.

  • Core pattern: token bucket per account + 429 with Retry-After + limit headers in response
  • Key trade-off: strict limits (system safe, lekin genuine bursts fail) vs generous limits (UX achha, lekin abuse risk)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: rate limit ko response headers me dikhao — client ko pata hona chahiye uski limit kya hai aur kab retry kare
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/rate-limiter
← PehleHow Shopify Handles Flash Sales at 32 Million Requests per MinuteAgla →How Stripe Prevents Double Payment Using Idempotent API
54System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

How Stripe Prevents Double Payment Using Idempotent API

Stripe ne idempotent API se double payment ki problem solve ki — ye payment systems ka sabse important pattern hai. Problem ye hai: network fail ho, client ko pata na chale payment hui ya nahi, wo dobara try kare — aur paise do baar kat jaayein.

Stripe ka solution hai Idempotency-Key header: client har request ke saath ek unique key bhejta hai. Stripe is key ko store karta hai — agar wahi key dobara aaye to Stripe pehli baar ka result hi wapas bhej deta hai, dobara charge nahi karta. Key ko 24 ghante tak store rakha jaata hai.

Is pattern ko implement karne ke liye server ko har idempotency key ke saath uska response bhi store karna hota hai — taaki retry par wahi response mil sake. Aur key ko request ke fingerprint se jodna hota hai: agar same key se alag amount ki request aaye to error milna chahiye, na ki purana response. Ye pattern sirf payments me nahi, har "exactly once" chahiye wali API me kaam aata hai.

  • Core pattern: Idempotency-Key header + key→response store (24h) + fingerprint validation
  • Key trade-off: key storage ka cost vs double-charge ka risk — payment me risk kabhi mat lo
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: network kabhi bhi fail ho sakta hai — isliye har payment API idempotent honi chahiye, retry safe hona chahiye
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/idempotent-api
← PehleThis Is How Stripe Does Rate Limiting to Build Scalable APIsAgla →Tumblr Shares Database Migration Strategy With 60+ Billion Rows
55System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

Tumblr Shares Database Migration Strategy With 60+ Billion Rows

Tumblr ne 60+ billion rows wale database ko migrate kiya — bina downtime ke. Itne bade database ka migration sabse mushkil operations me se ek hai, kyunki data copy karte-karte naya data bhi aata rehta hai.

Tumblr ki strategy thi dual-write + backfill + verification: pehle application ko dono databases (purana aur naya) me likhne ke liye badla gaya — dual-write. Phir purana data background me naye database me copy kiya gaya — backfill. Phir dono databases ke data ko compare karke verify kiya gaya ki sab kuch same hai. Jab confidence ho gaya to reads ko naye database par shift kiya gaya, aur purana band kar diya gaya.

Is process me sabse important hai rollback plan: agar naye database me kuch gadbad ho to turant purane par wapas jaana chahiye. Isliye migration ko steps me kiya gaya, har step par verification, aur har step reversible rakha gaya. Bade migrations me "big bang" kabhi mat karo — hamesha incremental, verified steps lo.

  • Key number: 60+ billion rows, zero-downtime migration
  • Core pattern: dual-write → backfill → verify → shift reads → decommission (har step reversible)
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: bade migration me sabse pehle rollback plan banao — agar wapas jaane ka rasta nahi hai to aage mat badho
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/how-to-migrate-a-mysql-database

56System Design Case Study — Real-World Case Studies

6 Proven Guidelines on Open Sourcing From Tumblr

Tumblr se open source karne ki 6 proven guidelines mili hain — ye engineering culture ka hissa hai, system design ka nahi, lekin har badi team ke liye zaroori hai. Open source karna sirf code public karna nahi hai — ye ek process hai.

Guidelines ka core hai: code ko pehle se hi open-source-ready likho — internal secrets, hardcoded URLs aur company-specific hacks code me na hon. Dusra: documentation aur examples code ke saath hone chahiye, nahi to koi use nahi karega. Teesra: maintainer ka time budget karo — open source project ko issues aur PRs ka jawab chahiye hota hai.

Chautha: license saaf honi chahiye, taaki logon ko pata ho wo code kaise use kar sakte hain. Paanchva: internal use pehle — jo code tum khud production me use karte ho, wahi open source karo, taaki wo tested rahe. Chhatha: community ko credit do — contributors ko recognize karo, taaki project zinda rahe.

  • Core pattern: open-source-ready code + docs + maintainer time + clear license + internal use first + contributor credit
  • Key trade-off: open source karna (community aur hiring me fayda, lekin maintenance cost) vs internal rakhna
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: open source ek product hai — code ke saath docs, license aur maintainer chahiye, nahi to wo mar jaata hai
Source:https://newsletter.systemdesign.one/p/open-source-guidelines

How Tinder Scaled to 1.6 Billion Swipes per Day

Tinder par har din 1.6 billion swipes hote hain — ye duniya ke sabse zyada write-heavy systems me se ek hai. Har swipe ek decision hai, aur use turant process karke agla profile dikhana hota hai.

Tinder ki architecture me recommendation engine central hai: kaunsa profile kisko dikhana hai, ye location, preferences, aur past swipes se tay hota hai. Profiles ko geospatial index me rakha jaata hai — paas wale log pehle dikhte hain. Swipe aate hi match check hota hai: agar dono ne ek dusre ko right swipe kiya to match banta hai aur dono ko notify kiya jaata hai.

1.6 billion writes per day ka matlab hai ~18,500 writes per second, average — peak par kai guna zyada. Isliye swipe data ko append-only log (Kafka) me likha jaata hai, aur match detection async hoti hai. Profile photos aur data ko CDN + cache se serve kiya jaata hai, taaki swipe karte waqt koi delay na ho.

  • Key number: 1.6 billion swipes per day (~18,500/sec average, peak kai guna)
  • Core pattern: geospatial index + append-only swipe log + async match detection + CDN for photos
  • Yaad rakhne wali baat: swipe jaise high-write features me write path ko bilkul halka rakho — match jaise heavy kaam async karo
